2-Coherent Internal Models of Homotopical Type Theory
Joshua Chen School of Computer Science, University of Nottingham

Abstract
The program of internal type theory seeks to develop the categorical model theory of dependent type theory using the language of dependent type theory itself. In the present work we study internal homotopical type theory by relaxing the notion of a category with families (cwf) to that of a wild , or precoherent higher cwf, and determine coherence conditions that suffice to recover properties expected of models of dependent type theory. The result is a definition of a split 2-coherent wild cwf , which admits as instances both the syntax and the “standard model” given by a universe type. This will allow us to give a straightforward internalization of the notion of a 2-coherent reflection of homotopical type theory in itself—namely as a 2-coherent wild cwf morphism from the syntax to the standard model. Our theory also easily specializes to give definitions of “low-dimensional” higher cwfs, and conjecturally includes the container higher model as a further instance.



\DeclareBibliographyOption

ibidpage[true]

1.  Introduction

1.1.  Internal type theory

Given a sufficiently expressive logical system
\mathfrak{L}
, it is interesting and productive to ask

To what extent does
\mathfrak{L}
internalize itself?

In more detail, one seeks to develop a suitable notion of
interpreting structure
, aka
model
, of
\mathfrak{L}
, and to study the theory of such models, entirely within the language and logic of
\mathfrak{L}
itself.
1
1

1

Immediate disclaimer: in this paper the term “model” always refers to this logical notion, and not to homotopy theoretic
model structures
.
There are, of course, models of homotopy type theory in model structures.

The techniques and perspectives granted by the study of these
inner models
have historically been used to surprising effect, e.g. to prove ZF-relative consistency of the axiom of choice and the continuum hypothesis
[
Göd38
]
, or to show independence of the Whitehead problem—a statement in homological algebra about short exact sequences of abelian groups—from the traditionally accepted set theoretic foundations of mathematics
[
She74
]
.

The program of
internal type theory
, first articulated by Dybjer
[
Dyb96
]
, seeks to develop the same paradigm in the setting of dependent type theory
2
2

2

For conciseness, henceforth simply “type theory”.

by studying the categorical model theory of intensional Martin-Löf type theory (MLTT) using MLTT. Among the type theory community, this is also known as
internal model theory
of type theory.

The foundational task of internal type theory is then to give a type theoretic definition of the notion of a categorical “model” of type theory, ensuring that it captures all the examples we care about.
In early work, Buisse and Dybjer
[
BD08
]
formalize the type of
1-categories with families
(
1-cwfs
)
[
Dyb96
,
Hof97
]
in MLTT, and discuss approaches to constructing term models and the initial cwf.
Later, Ahrens, Lumsdaine and Voevodsky
[
ALV18
]
consider a number of other kinds of model in the same setting,
3
3

3

On occasion, assuming univalence.

and show the equivalence of 1-cwfs with
(split) type categories
[
BG12
,
Pit95
,
Pit01
]
(aka categories with attributes
[
Mog91
,
Hof97
]
)
and representable natural transformations
(aka natural models
[
Awo18
]
),
via relative universes.

Two structures that we would particularly like to exhibit as internal models of a type theory are its
syntax
and, if present, its
universe type
.
Altenkirch and Kaposi
[
AK16
]
show that if MLTT with uniqueness of identity proofs (UIP) is extended with
quotient inductive-inductive types
(
QIITs
)
[
Alt+18
,
KKA19
]
then the strongly typed term-cwf sketched by Buisse and Dybjer
[
BD08
, §6.1]
can be internally constructed, thus yielding an internal syntactic model.
Even better, from this inductive construction it immediately follows that internal 1-cwfs satisfy the
initiality principle
, which posits that syntax should give rise to an initial object in an appropriate (higher) category of models. Assuming UIP also allows any universe type in MLTT to be equipped with a canonical 1-cwf structure, giving rise to an internal model known in the literature as the
standard model

[
AK16
,
Kra21
]
.

Now, for any internal definition of model of MLTT which includes as instances both the syntax
\mathcal{S}
and a universe
\mathcal{U}
, we may ask if the type of model morphisms from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathcal{U}
is definable and inhabited.
Elements of such a type may be viewed as
self-interpretations
, or
reflections
of MLTT in itself.
4
4

4

The self-interpretation—as opposed to just the internal
representation
—of type theory is a fascinating topic of study; Rendel, Ostermann and Hofer
[
ROH09
, §2]
give an excellent account of the subtle distinctions involved, albeit in the context of typed lambda calculi.

By the preceding discussion, in MLTT+
\mathcal{U}
+UIP+QIITs the type of 1-cwf morphisms from the syntax to the standard universe model is definable, and moreover inhabited by eliminating the syntax into the universe.
We may colloquially summarize all of this by saying that any model
5
5

5

Taking 1-cwfs, or any of the equivalent notions mentioned previously.

of MLTT+
\mathcal{U}
+UIP+QIITs has an inner syntactic model
\mathcal{S}
of its MLTT fragment, as well as an internalized reflection function
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{U}
.

1.2.  Internal homotopical type theory

Can we tell a similar story about inner models of
homotopical
MLTT, i.e. without assuming UIP?
Unfortunately, the account given in the previous section depends heavily on the uniqueness of identity proofs—both for the semantics of QIITs, but also for the interpretation of the universe as an internal model.
In particular, it is well known that types with their higher identities possess
\infty
-groupoidal
structure in the absence of UIP
[
Lum10
,
BG11
,
KL21
]
, which means that the canonical standard model on the universe
\mathcal{U}
is no longer a 1-cwf since its substitutions
\mathcal{U}(A,B)\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}A\to B
are not generally sets.

We must therefore return to the foundational task of internal type theory, and ask what a good notion of internal model of homotopical type theory might look like.
This question is considered by Kraus
[
Kra21
]
in the setting of a two-level type theory (2LTT)
[
ACKS23
]
that extends homotopical MLTT with an additional layer of
strict
, aka
non-fibrant
, types, including an equality type former satisfying UIP.
With this extra structure it is now possible to define
\infty
-categories as semi-Segal types
[
CK17
]
having idempotent equivalences
[
Kra21
, §III]
; to answer the question of models by defining
\infty
-categories with families
; and to show that the syntax QIIT, the universe standard model, and any slice of an
\infty
-cwf are all
\infty
-cwfs.

However, models of 2LTT are somewhat strong extensions of models of MLTT, and it might therefore be argued that
\infty
-cwfs should not be considered to be inner models of homotopical MLTT in itself.
6
6

6

Of course, even the account we gave of inner models of MLTT+UIP in the previous section does not, strictly speaking, provide models of MLTT+UIP in itself.
One source of tension in inner model theory is to make the gap between the theory of the outer and inner models (the “host” theory and the “object” theory) as small as possible.
How large this gap is allowed to be is typically a matter of some subjective judgment.

Indeed, Kraus conjectures
[
Kra21
, §VI]
that plain homotopy type theory (HoTT)
[
Uni13
]
does not internalize itself, but instead considers the definition of
\infty
-cwfs to be a step towards showing that 2LTT internalizes HoTT and also itself.

Another potential approach is to work in the axiomatic variation of
simplicial homotopy type theory

[
RS17
,
RS23
]
developed by Gratzer, Weinberger and Buchholtz
[
GWB24
]
, in which it is possible to give a straightforward definition of (
\infty
, 1)-categories as Segal or Rezk types.
However, in this theory representable presheaves are defined using additional modalities
[
GWB25
]
, again making it a rather strong extension of plain HoTT.

1.3.  Contributions

Thus, in the present work we stick with the original question and fix homotopical MLTT to be the theory of our outer models (i.e. the theory in which we perform our constructions).
Since defining a type of
\infty
-categories and, a fortiori,
\infty
-categorical models in this theory remains an open problem, we take the notion of a
wild
, or
precoherent higher
category with families as our starting point for “internal model of homotopical MLTT” (
Section

4
).
We then determine coherence conditions such that sufficiently coherent internal cwfs satisfy many good properties expected of any model of type theory (
Section

5
).
In particular, we show that requiring
2-coherence
suffices to equip any wild cwf
\mathcal{C}
with the expected cloven fibrational structure, which furthermore satisfies a coherent “splitting” property when the category of contexts of
\mathcal{C}
is either set-level or univalent (
Section

6
).
This generalizes the well known result that 1-cwfs are equivalent to full split comprehension categories.
In order to do this, we first have to develop some wild category theory (
Section

2
), as well as a theory of pullbacks in wild categories (
Section

3
).

The benefit of our approach is that we are able to capture, in a single internally definable notion, both set-level models such as the syntax as well as untruncated higher models such as the universe.
We are then able to prove results simultaneously for all such models by stating them as generally as possible in terms of coherence, without resorting to truncation assumptions or restricting to some homotopy
n
-level.
Our theory is still easily applicable to models with “low-dimensional” higher homotopy: in particular, the simple generalization of the theory of 1-cwfs to allow the type presheaf to be valued in 1-types is (almost trivially) an instance of our theory.

1.4.  Assumptions and conventions

Logical setting

Our definitions and constructions are in the setting of MLTT with
\Pi
,
\Sigma
and intensional identity types, without assuming uniqueness of identity proofs.
We assume function extensionality as well as
\upeta
for
\Pi
-types throughout.
We do not globally assume univalence, but instead take it to be a property that may or may not hold for a given universe type.

Univalent 1-category theory

Of the reader, we assume familiarity with the basics of homotopy type theory as well as the theory of 1-categories in univalent foundations, standard references for which are
[
Uni13
]
and
[
AKS15
]
.

Transport

We frequently work with explicit transports over complicated path concatenations, and use the notation

a{}_{\,\downarrow}{{}^{P}_{\,{e}}}

to denote the transport of an element
a\mathrel{:}P(x)
along an equality
e\mathrel{:}x=y
.
We will also use, without explicit comment, the equation

a{}_{\,\downarrow}{{}^{P}_{\,{e}}}{}_{\,\downarrow}{{}^{P}_{\,{{e}^{\prime}}}}=a{}_{\,\downarrow}{{}^{P}_{\,{e\mathbin{\cdot}{e}^{\prime}}}}\,,

which holds by
[
Uni13
, Lemma 2.3.9]
.

2.  Wild Categories

To internalize categorical models of homotopical type theory we first have to define some type of suitable category-like structure that captures both the 1-categorical syntax as well as the
\infty
-categorical universe.
Until the problem of defining general
\infty
-categorical structures using only features of homotopical MLTT is solved,
we consider the approximation given by
wild
, or
precoherent higher
, categories.
Variations on this notion appear a number of times in the literature and formalization libraries
[
CK17
,
HH24
,
RSPB+
,
Bau+
]
, typically as an intermediate stage toward defining more coherent structures.
We use a particularly simple definition: they are almost precategories
[
AKS15
, Definition 3.1]
, except that morphisms form
types
instead of sets.

Definition 2.0.1.

Wild categories
.

A
wild
or
precoherent higher category

\mathcal{C}
consists of:

❂

A type
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
of
objects
.

❂

For all
x,y\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, a
type

\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
of
morphisms
from
x
to
y
.

❂

An
composition
operation
\mathbin{\diamond}
of compatible morphisms, together with an
associator

\alpha
witnessing associativity of composition

(h\mathbin{\diamond}g)\mathbin{\diamond}f{\mathrel{\to}^{\alpha_{f,g,h}}}h\mathbin{\diamond}g\mathbin{\diamond}f

for all morphisms
f
,
g
,
h
.

❂

Identity morphisms

\mathrm{id}_{x}
for all
x\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, together with
unitors

\lambda
and
\rho
witnessing the left and right identity equations

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}_{y}\mathbin{\diamond}f{\mathrel{\to}^{\lambda_{f}}}f

\displaystyle f\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id}_{x}{\mathrel{\to}^{\rho_{f}}}f

for all
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
.

Crucially, no further coherence laws on hom-types are required in the definition of a wild category.
We frequently leave the indices on the associator, unitors and identity morphisms implicit.

Examples 2.0.2.

Wild categories
.

We are primarily interested in two particular kinds of wild category, which are in fact completely coherent.
These are distinguished by the behavior of their hom-types: the “maximally truncated” and the “nontrivially fully coherent”.

The first kind consists of the wild categories whose morphisms form sets: any precategory—and hence, set-level or univalent 1-category—is immediately a wild category.

The second kind consists of the type theoretic (sub-) universes.
Any universe type
\mathcal{U}
gives rise to a wild category, also denoted
\mathcal{U}
, with objects
{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathcal{U}
, and whose hom-types
\mathcal{U}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{B})
are the function types
A\to B
.
Composition is given by function composition and identity morphisms by identity functions.
The associativity and unit laws hold definitionally, i.e.
\alpha
,
\lambda
and
\rho
are families of trivial equations.
This definition applies equally well to any reflective subuniverse
[
Uni13
, Definition 7.7.1]
,
7
7

7

This should also straightforwardly yield a notion of “wild reflective subcategory”; we do not develop this in this paper.

and in this way the
m
-modal types for any modality
m
on a universe
\mathcal{U}

[
Uni13
,
RSS20
]
form a wild category.
In particular, we have wild categories whose objects are the
n
-types in
\mathcal{U}
, which might be seen as prototypical examples of wild
(
n
, 1)
-categories.

We draw diagrams in wild categories after the familiar notation, with one important difference.
Since morphisms in a wild category can form arbitrary types, the commutativity of a given face of a diagram is no longer property, but
data
.
Hence we denote commuting faces with the notation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle\,\gamma

to make explicit the commutativity witness
\gamma\mathrel{:}f=g
.
As this notation suggests, equalities between morphisms in a wild category are considered to be
2-cells
, and higher equalities,
higher cells
.
We stress that these cells are not explicitly axiomatized as part of the generalized algebraic theory of a wild category, but instead arise out of the ambient homotopical type theory.

Remark 2.0.3.

On the semantics of wild categories
.

The fact that a wild category is a proper generalization of a precategory therefore relies crucially on the homotopical nature of the identity type.
This simple observation suggests that we distinguish the
categorical
and
typal
directions of a wild category: the 1-cells belong to the categorical direction, and the higher cells to the typal.
This recalls the point of view—implicit in Rezk
[
Rez01
]
and explicated by Joyal and Tierney
[
JT07
]
—that Segal spaces (i.e. bisimplicial sets
{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set}
satisfying the Segal condition) have categorical and
spatial
directions.
Indeed, Capriotti and Kraus
[
CK17
, Theorem 4.9]
show (a version of) this to hold.
8
8

8

By interpreting their type theoretic proof in the simplicial model of HoTT
[
KL21
]
.

Alternatively, following Lumsdaine
[
Lum10
]
and van den Berg and Garner
[
BG11
]
in viewing types as Batanin-Leinster weak
\omega
-groupoids, it should also be possible to make precise the point of view that wild categories are categories “wildly enriched” in weak
\omega
-groupoids.
9
9

9

That is, weakly enriched, but without requiring any coherences on
n
-cells for
n\geq 2
.

2.1.  Common concepts

Expectedly, a large number of elementary concepts from univalent 1-category theory
[
AKS15
]
and bicategory theory
[
Ahr+21
]
straightforwardly transfer into, and are subsumed by, the wild categorical setting.
We record them here, as well as in
Sections

2.2
and
2.3
, for completeness and future reference.

Definition 2.1.1.

Terminal objects
.

An object
y
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
is
terminal
if
\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
is contractible for all objects
x\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.

Definition 2.1.2.

Sections and retractions
.

The type of
sections
of a morphism
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
is

\mathrm{Sect}(f)\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(s\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{x}))$}}\hskip 1.0pt,{\,{f\mathbin{\diamond}s=\mathrm{id}}}.

Similarly, the type of
retractions
of
f
is

\mathrm{Retr}(f)\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(r\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{x}))$}}\hskip 1.0pt,{\,{r\mathbin{\diamond}f=\mathrm{id}}}.

In contrast to 1-categories, sections and retractions in wild categories are not solely determined by their morphism component in general, but also by the identification of the section-retraction composite with the identity.

Definition 2.1.3.

Whiskering
.

Given an equality
\gamma\mathrel{:}g={g}^{\prime}
of morphisms
g,{g}^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
, for any morphism
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({w},\hskip 1.5pt{x})
the
right whiskering

(\gamma\mathbin{\ast}f)
of
\gamma
with
f
is the canonical equality

\mathrm{ap}\,{(\_\mathbin{\diamond}f)}\,{\gamma}\mathrel{:}g\mathbin{\diamond}f={g}^{\prime}\mathbin{\diamond}f,

and for any
h\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{z})
the
left whiskering

(h\mathbin{\ast}\gamma)
of
\gamma
with
h
is the equality

\mathrm{ap}\,{(h\mathbin{\diamond}\_)}\,{\gamma}\mathrel{:}h\mathbin{\diamond}g=h\mathbin{\diamond}{g}^{\prime}.

Proposition 2.1.4.

Properties of whiskering
.

By induction, the following equations hold for right whiskering:

\displaystyle\mathrm{refl}\mathbin{\ast}f\equiv\mathrm{refl},

\displaystyle\gamma\mathbin{\ast}\mathrm{id}=\rho\mathbin{\cdot}\gamma\mathbin{\cdot}{\rho}^{-1},

\displaystyle{(\gamma\mathbin{\ast}f)}^{-1}={\gamma}^{-1}\mathbin{\ast}f,

\displaystyle(\gamma\mathbin{\cdot}\delta)\mathbin{\ast}f=(\gamma\mathbin{\ast}f)\mathbin{\cdot}(\delta\mathbin{\ast}f),

as well as the analogous equations for left whiskering.
We also have the following associativity laws expressing “naturality” of
\alpha
,

\displaystyle g\mathbin{\ast}(f\mathbin{\ast}\gamma)={\alpha}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}((g\mathbin{\diamond}f)\mathbin{\ast}\gamma)\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha,

\displaystyle(\gamma\mathbin{\ast}g)\mathbin{\ast}f=\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}(\gamma\mathbin{\ast}(g\mathbin{\diamond}f))\mathbin{\cdot}{\alpha}^{-1},

\displaystyle(g\mathbin{\ast}\gamma)\mathbin{\ast}f=\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}(g\mathbin{\ast}(\gamma\mathbin{\ast}f))\mathbin{\cdot}{\alpha}^{-1},

and the interchange law

(g\mathbin{\ast}\gamma)\mathbin{\cdot}(\delta\mathbin{\ast}{f}^{\prime})=(\delta\mathbin{\ast}f)\mathbin{\cdot}({g}^{\prime}\mathbin{\ast}\gamma)

for all morphisms
f
,
{f}^{\prime}
,
g
,
{g}^{\prime}
and equalities
\gamma
,
\delta
as in

2.2.  2-coherence

The following two coherence conditions are familiar from bicategory theory.

Definition 2.2.1.

Triangle coherators
.

A wild category
\mathcal{C}
has
triangle coherators
if for all morphisms

x\xrightarrow{f}y\xrightarrow{g}z

in
\mathcal{C}
, there is an equality

\triangle_{{f},\,{g}}\mathrel{:}\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}(g\mathbin{\ast}\lambda)=\rho\mathbin{\ast}f

filling the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id})\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\diamond}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\mathbin{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\ast}\lambda}

Definition 2.2.2.

Pentagonators
.

\mathcal{C}
has
pentagon coherators for associators
, or
(
\alpha
-) pentagonators
, if for all composable chains

v\xrightarrow{f}w\xrightarrow{g}x\xrightarrow{h}y\xrightarrow{k}z

of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, there is an equality

\pentagon_{{f},\,{g},\,{h},\,{k}}\mathrel{:}(\alpha\mathbin{\ast}f)\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}(k\mathbin{\ast}\alpha)=\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha

filling the usual pentagon

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((k\mathbin{\diamond}h)\mathbin{\diamond}g)\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k\mathbin{\diamond}h\mathbin{\diamond}g)\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k\mathbin{\diamond}h)\mathbin{\diamond}g\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\mathbin{\diamond}(h\mathbin{\diamond}g)\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\mathbin{\diamond}h\mathbin{\diamond}g\mathbin{\diamond}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathbin{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\mathbin{\ast}\alpha}

Definition 2.2.3.

2-coherent wild categories
.

We call a wild category
2-coherent
if it has triangle and pentagon coherators.

In the literature, Hart and Hou
[
HH24
]
call 2-coherent wild categories
bicategories
; we do not use this terminology as 2-coherent wild categories always have invertible 2-cells (and higher cells). 2-coherent wild categories are also essentially the
wild 2-precategories
of Capriotti and Kraus
[
CK17
]
; the difference is that their definition also includes the other two triangle coherators of
Proposition

2.2.6
in the data of the type.
We also have the following link to the univalent bicategory theory of Ahrens et al.
[
Ahr+21
]
:

Proposition 2.2.4.

Any 2-coherent wild category is also a
prebicategory
in the sense of Ahrens et al.
[
Ahr+21
, Definition 2.1]
, by taking the type of 2-cells from
f
to
g
to be the equality type
f=g
, and using
Proposition

2.1.4
.

Examples 2.2.5.

2-coherent higher categories
.

Any precategory trivially satisfies all higher equalities between equalities of morphisms.
The universe wild categories
\mathcal{U}
have definitionally unital and associative composition of morphisms, and thus have trivial triangle and pentagon coherators.

Many coherences
involving
\lambda
,
\rho
and
\alpha
that hold in all bicategories
also hold in 2-coherent wild categories—namely, those that do not rely on uniqueness of equality of 2-cells.
In particular,

Proposition 2.2.6.

In a 2-coherent wild category
\mathcal{C}
, there are witnesses (not necessarily unique) that

❂

\lambda_{\mathrm{id}_{x}}=\rho_{\mathrm{id}_{x}}
for all
x\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, and

❂

the diagrams of equalities

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}g)\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}g\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\diamond}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\mathbin{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\mathbin{\diamond}f)\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\diamond}f\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\diamond}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\ast}\rho}

commute for all
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
and
g\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{z})
.

We refer to
[
JY21
, Propositions 2.2.4 and 2.2.6]
for proofs of these facts.

Non-example 2.2.7.

A wild category lacking 2-coherators
.

In
[
Kra21a
, Lemma 8]
, Kraus uses the circle higher inductive type to construct a wild category
\mathcal{E}
with an object
x\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
that refutes
\lambda_{\mathrm{id}_{x}}=\rho_{\mathrm{id}_{x}}
.
By
Proposition

2.2.6
,
\mathcal{E}
must therefore also fail to have triangle or pentagon coherators.
In fact, one can see directly by
[
Uni13
, Lemma 6.4.2]
that the existence of triangle coherators for
\mathcal{E}
is a HoTT taboo.

We will also use the following coherence.

Proposition 2.2.8.

Suppose that
\gamma\mathrel{:}g=\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f
in a 2-coherent wild category.
Then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

commutes, i.e.
\lambda_{g}\mathbin{\cdot}\gamma=_{(\mathrm{id}\,\mathbin{\diamond}\,g\,=\,\mathrm{id}\,\mathbin{\diamond}\,f)}(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\gamma)\mathbin{\cdot}(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\lambda_{f})
.

Proof.

We equivalently prove that
\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\gamma=\lambda_{g}\mathbin{\cdot}\gamma\mathbin{\cdot}{(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\lambda_{f})}^{-1}
.
Since
\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\gamma=\lambda_{g}\mathbin{\cdot}\gamma\mathbin{\cdot}{\lambda}^{-1}_{\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}
by properties of whiskering (
Proposition

2.1.4
), the result follows if
\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\lambda_{f}=\lambda_{\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}
.

Diagram

2.2.8.1
shows how to construct such an equality: its interior is divided into two triangles and a bigon, which commute by the triangle coherator
\triangle_{{f},\,{\mathrm{id}}}
and the equalities in
Proposition

2.2.6
.

Diagram 2.2.8.1:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}\mathrm{id})\mathbin{\diamond}f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}\lambda_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathrm{id}}\mathbin{\ast}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathrm{id}}\mathbin{\ast}f}

∎

2.3.  Equivalence and univalence

Definition 2.3.1.

Wild equivalences
.

A morphism
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
is a
wild equivalence
if it has both a section and a retraction (i.e. is biinvertible).
The type of wild equivalences from
x
to
y
in
\mathcal{C}
is denoted
{x}\simeq_{\mathcal{C}}{y}
.
Its elements are also called
\mathcal{C}
-equivalences
to avoid confusion with type theoretic equivalences.

The following observation appears as a basic result in the classical theory of
\infty
-categories
[
Lur09
,
Har15
]
.
To our knowledge, its type theoretic incarnation is due to Capriotti and Kraus, who also observe the immediate corollary that being a wild equivalence is a proposition
[
CK17
, Lemma 5.3]
.

Proposition 2.3.2.

The universal property of wild equivalences
.

A morphism
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
is a
\mathcal{C}
-equivalence if and only if it is
neutral
—i.e. if and only if for any
w,z\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, the maps

\displaystyle f\mathbin{\diamond}\_

\displaystyle\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({w},\hskip 1.5pt{x})\to\mathcal{C}({w},\hskip 1.5pt{y})

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\_\mathbin{\diamond}f

\displaystyle\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{z})\to\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{z})

are equivalences of hom-types.

Proof.

Let
s
and
r
be, respectively, a section and retraction of
f
in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then
(s\mathbin{\diamond}\_)
and
(r\mathbin{\diamond}\_)
are, respectively, sections and retractions of
(f\mathbin{\diamond}\_)
, while
(\_\mathbin{\diamond}r)
and
(\_\mathbin{\diamond}s)
are, respectively, sections and retractions of
(\_\mathbin{\diamond}f)
.

Conversely, the inverse equivalences of
(f\mathbin{\diamond}\_)\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{x})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
and
(\_\mathbin{\diamond}f)\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{x})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{x})
map identity morphisms to a section and retraction of
f
, respectively.
∎

Definition 2.3.3.

Wild isomorphisms
.

We also consider the type of wild
isomorphisms

{x}\cong_{\mathcal{C}}{y}
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the type of morphisms
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y})
having a two-sided inverse
g\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({y},\hskip 1.5pt{x})
.

Any two-sided inverse is both a section and a retraction, so there is a canonical map

\mathrm{isotoeqv}_{\mathcal{C}}\mathrel{:}({x}\cong_{\mathcal{C}}{y})\to({x}\simeq_{\mathcal{C}}{y})

for any objects
x,y\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is a precategory then
\mathrm{isotoeqv}_{\mathcal{C}}
is an equivalence: its inverse sends a section-retraction pair
(s,r)
to the two-sided inverse
(r\mathbin{\diamond}f\mathbin{\diamond}s)
of
f
.

Definition 2.3.4.

Dependent identity morphisms
.

If
x,y\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
are objects of a wild category such that
e\mathrel{:}x=y
, there is a morphism
10
10

10

\mathrm{idd}
for “
id
entity morphism
d
ependent over an equality”.

\mathrm{idd}(e)\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{id}_{x}{}_{\,\downarrow}{{}^{\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{\_})}_{\,{e}}}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({x},\hskip 1.5pt{y}).

By induction on
e
,
\mathrm{idd}(e)
is an isomorphism, with inverse
\mathrm{idd}({e}^{-1})
.
We thus get a map

\mathrm{idd}\mathrel{:}x=y\to{x}\cong_{\mathcal{C}}{y}.

In precategories,
\mathrm{idd}
is essentially
\mathrm{idtoiso}

[
Uni13
,
AKS15
]
.

Definition 2.3.5.

Wild univalence
.

A wild category
\mathcal{C}
is
univalent
if, for all
x,y\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, the map

\mathrm{idtoeqv}_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\mathrm{isotoeqv}_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbin{\circ}\mathrm{idd}\mathrel{:}x=y\to{x}\simeq_{\mathcal{C}}{y}

is an equivalence.
Its inverse equivalence is denoted
\mathrm{eqvtoid}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Wild univalence subsumes both 1-categorical univalence (when
\mathcal{C}
is a precategory) and the univalence axiom (when
\mathcal{C}\equiv\mathcal{U}
is a universe).

3.  Pullbacks in 2-Coherent Wild Categories

In this section we develop the theory of pullbacks in 2-coherent wild categories.
These jointly generalize comma objects in (2, 1)-categories (including 1-categorical pullbacks) as well as type theoretic homotopy pullbacks
[
AKL15
]
.

3.1.  Commuting squares

Definition 3.1.1.

Cospans
.

A
cospan
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
is an element of

\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{C})\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(A,B,C\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0})$}}{\,{(f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{C}))\,(g\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({B},\hskip 1.5pt{C}))}}.

We denote cospans graphically as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 3.01389ptg}
,
or simply by
(f,g)
when the vertices are understood.

Definition 3.1.2.

Commuting squares
.

A
commuting square
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
consists of

❂

a
cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 3.01389ptg}
,

❂

a
source
object
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
,

❂

two
legs

m_{A}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{A})
and
m_{B}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{B})
,

❂

and an
equality 2-cell

\gamma\mathrel{:}f\mathbin{\diamond}m_{A}=g\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B}

as in the following diagram in
\mathcal{C}
,

First, we consider the type of commuting squares on a cospan with fixed source.

Definition 3.1.3.

Commuting squares, with fixed source and cospan
.

Indexing over cospans

\mathfrak{c}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hbox to89.45pt{\vbox to13.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.72469pt\lower-3.5165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

and source objects
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
,
we define the type

\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(m_{A}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{A}))\,(m_{B}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{B}))$}}{\,{(\gamma\mathrel{:}f\mathbin{\diamond}m_{A}=g\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B})}}

of
commuting squares on
\mathfrak{c}
with source
X
.

Avigad, Kapulkin and Lumsdaine
[
AKL15
]
also call
\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)
(instantiated in universes) the type of
cones
over
\mathfrak{c}
with vertex
X
.

Characterizing the equality of
\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)
is routine.

Equality of
\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)
.

be a cospan,
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
an object, and

\mathfrak{S}\equiv(m_{A},m_{B},\gamma)\hskip 6.45831pt\text{and}\hskip 6.45831pt{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\equiv({m_{A}}^{\prime},{m_{B}}^{\prime},{\gamma}^{\prime})

be elements of
\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)
in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
.
Then the equality type
\mathfrak{S}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(e_{A}\mathrel{:}m_{A}={m_{A}}^{\prime})\,(e_{B}\mathrel{:}m_{B}={m_{B}}^{\prime})$}}\hskip 1.0pt,{\,{\gamma=(f\mathbin{\ast}e_{A})\mathbin{\cdot}{\gamma}^{\prime}\mathbin{\cdot}{(g\mathbin{\ast}e_{B})}^{-1}}}.

Proof.

By a routine application of the fundamental theorem of identity types
[
Rij22
, Theorem 11.2.2]
, the algebra of
\Sigma
-types, and
Proposition

2.1.4
.
∎

We have the following operations on commuting squares.

Definition 3.1.5.

Transpose
.

For any cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 3.01389ptg}

and
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, the
transpose
map

{\_}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathrel{:}\mathrm{CommSq}_{(f,g)}(X)\to\mathrm{CommSq}_{(g,f)}(X)

is given by

{(m_{A},m_{B},\gamma)}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}(m_{B},m_{A},{\gamma}^{-1}).

Proposition 3.1.6.

Transpose is an equivalence
.

Transpose is involutive:
(\mathfrak{S}^{\mathrm{T}})^{\mathrm{T}}=\mathfrak{S}
for all
\mathfrak{S}
, and so in particular
{\_}^{\mathrm{T}}
is an equivalence.

Definition 3.1.7.

Horizontal and vertical pasting
.

From a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}

of commuting squares
\mathfrak{Q}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}(i,{f}^{\prime},\mathfrak{q})
and
\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}(j,{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{p})
we get the

\frac{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hskip 4.30554pt{\alpha}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}(\mathfrak{p}\mathbin{\ast}{f}^{\prime})\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}(g\mathbin{\ast}\mathfrak{q})\mathbin{\cdot}{\alpha}^{-1}
.

Definition

3.1.7
is in some ways redundant: a straightforward calculation shows that

\frac{\mathfrak{Q}}{\mathfrak{P}}=({{\mathfrak{Q}}^{\mathrm{T}}}\mid{{\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathrm{T}}})^{\mathrm{T}},

and so one could simply vertically paste squares by horizontally pasting their transposes.
However, this equality is only propositional, and it turns out to be more convenient later to use the canonical form of the vertical pasting as we have defined it.

Definition 3.1.8.

Vertical pasting map
.

For any
A\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
,
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{B})
and
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, the vertical pasting with

\mathfrak{P}\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hskip 4.30554pt\hbox to80.2pt{\vbox to52.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.10063pt\lower-27.04419pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

yields a map

\mathrm{CommSq}_{(j,f)}(X)\to\mathrm{CommSq}_{(k,g\mathbin{\diamond}f)}(X)
.
That is, we have the family

\frac{\textnormal{--}}{\mathfrak{P}}\mathrel{:}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Pi$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(A\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0})\,(f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{B}))\,(X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0})$}}\;{\mathrm{CommSq}_{(j,f)}(X)\to\mathrm{CommSq}_{(k,g\mathbin{\diamond}f)}(X)}.

Morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
act contravariantly on commuting squares by precomposition at their source.

Definition 3.1.9.

.

For any cospan
\mathfrak{c}\equiv\hbox to89.45pt{\vbox to13.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 44.72469pt\lower-3.5165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

and
X,Y\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, there is a
precomposition
map

\_\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}\_\mathrel{:}\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(Y)\to\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{Y})\to\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)

defined by

(m_{A},m_{B},\gamma)\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}m\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hskip 4.30554pt(m_{A}\mathbin{\diamond}m,\ m_{B}\mathbin{\diamond}m,\ {\alpha}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}(\gamma\mathbin{\ast}m)\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha)
.

Lemma 3.1.10.

Right action of morphisms on commuting squares
.

If
\mathfrak{S}\mathrel{:}\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)
is a commuting square in a 2-coherent wild category then

\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}\mathrm{id}_{X}=\mathfrak{S},

and for all
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{Y})
and
g\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({Y},\hskip 1.5pt{Z})
,

\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}(g\mathbin{\diamond}f)=\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}g\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}f.

Proof.

By calculation, properties of whiskering (
Proposition

2.1.4
), and coherence—namely, the right identity triangle coherence (
Proposition

2.2.6
) for the first claim, and the pentagon coherence for the second.
∎

Corollary 3.1.11.

By induction on
e
and
Lemma

3.1.10
,

\mathfrak{S}{}_{\,\downarrow}{{}^{\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\_)}_{\,{e}}}=_{\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}({X}^{\prime})}\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}\mathrm{idd}({e}^{-1})

for every
\mathfrak{S}\mathrel{:}\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)
and
e\mathrel{:}X={X}^{\prime}
.

Now we can characterize the identity of commuting squares on a cospan, allowing their source objects to vary.

Definition 3.1.12.

Commuting squares on a cospan
.

The type of commuting squares on a cospan
\mathfrak{c}
is the total space

\mathrm{CommSq}(\mathfrak{c})\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0})$}}{\,{\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)}}.

Corollary 3.1.13.

Equality of
\mathrm{CommSq}(\mathfrak{c})
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathfrak{S})
and
({X}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
are two commuting squares on a cospan
\mathfrak{c}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}(f,g)
.
The equality

(X,\mathfrak{S})=_{\mathrm{CommSq}(\mathfrak{c})}({X}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

is equivalent to

\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(e\mathrel{:}X={X}^{\prime})$}}\hskip 1.0pt,{\,{\mathfrak{S}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}\mathrm{idd}(e)}}.

Proof.

By the equality of
\Sigma
-types and
Corollary

3.1.11
.
∎

Up to this point we have considered commuting squares with respect to a fixed cospan

(A,B,C,f,g)\mathrel{:}\mathrm{Cospan}(\mathcal{C})
.
We will also need to compare squares on cospans with propositionally equal but definitionally distinct legs
(f,g)
and
({f}^{\prime},{g}^{\prime})
.
To this end, we index the type of commuting squares over their vertices, and consider the following type family.

Definition 3.1.14.

Commuting squares, with fixed vertices
.

For
X,A,B,C\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, define

\mathrm{CommSq}(X,A,B,C)\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{C}))\,(g\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({B},\hskip 1.5pt{C}))$}}\hskip 1.0pt,{\,{\mathrm{CommSq}_{(f,g)}(X)}}.

That is,
\mathrm{CommSq}(X,A,B,C)
is the total space of
\mathrm{CommSq}_{(A,B,C,f,g)}(X)
fibered over
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{C})
and
g\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({B},\hskip 1.5pt{C})
.

We characterize the identity type of
\mathrm{CommSq}(X,A,B,C)
using the following version of Rijke’s structure identity principle
[
Rij22
, Theorem 11.6.2]
, which may be understood as a dependent form of the fundamental theorem of identity types.

Theorem 3.1.15.

Structure identity principle
.

Suppose that
A
is a type pointed at
a\mathrel{:}A
, and
B\mathrel{:}A\to\mathcal{U}
is a type family over
A
, pointed at
b\mathrel{:}B(a)
.
Then for any type family

R\mathrel{:}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Pi$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(x\mathrel{:}A)$}}\;{a=x\to B(x)\to\mathcal{U}}

pointed at
r\mathrel{:}R(a,\mathrm{refl}_{a},b)
, the canonical family of maps indexed over
x
and
y

\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Pi$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(x\mathrel{:}A)\,(y\mathrel{:}B(x))$}}\;{(a,b)=(x,y)\to\Sigma\,{(p\mathrel{:}a=x)},{\,{R(x,p,y)}}}

is a family of equivalences if and only if the total space
\Sigma\,{(B(a))}{\,{R(a,\mathrm{refl}_{a})}}
is contractible.

Lemma 3.1.16.

Equality of
\mathrm{CommSq}(X,A,B,C)
.

Let

(f,g,\mathfrak{S}),\ ({f}^{\prime},{g}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})\mathrel{:}\mathrm{CommSq}(X,A,B,C)

be commuting squares with vertices
X,A,B,C\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, where
\mathfrak{S}\equiv(m_{A},m_{B},\gamma)
and
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\equiv({m_{A}}^{\prime},{m_{B}}^{\prime},{\gamma}^{\prime})
.
The equality
(f,g,\mathfrak{S})=({f}^{\prime},{g}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(e_{f}\mathrel{:}f={f}^{\prime})\,(e_{g}\mathrel{:}g={g}^{\prime})\,(e_{A}\mathrel{:}m_{A}={m_{A}}^{\prime})\,(e_{B}\mathrel{:}m_{B}={m_{B}}^{\prime})$}}\hskip 1.0pt,

\displaystyle\gamma=(e_{f}\mathbin{\ast}m_{A})\mathbin{\cdot}({f}^{\prime}\mathbin{\ast}e_{A})\mathbin{\cdot}{\gamma}^{\prime}\mathbin{\cdot}{({g}^{\prime}\mathbin{\ast}e_{B})}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}{(e_{g}\mathbin{\ast}m_{B})}^{-1}.

This last component is a proof that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\mathbin{\diamond}m_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}^{\prime}\mathbin{\diamond}m_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}^{\prime}\mathbin{\diamond}{m_{A}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}^{\prime}\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}^{\prime}\mathbin{\diamond}{m_{B}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{f}\mathbin{\ast}m_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f}^{\prime}\mathbin{\ast}e_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{g}\mathbin{\ast}m_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}^{\prime}\mathbin{\ast}e_{B}}

commutes.

Proof.

Use the structure identity principle (
Theorem

3.1.15
) applied to the pointed type

(\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{C})\times\mathcal{C}({B},\hskip 1.5pt{C}),\ (f,g))

and the type family

\mathrm{CommSq}_{(A,B,C,\,\_\,,\,\_)}(X)\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{C})\times\mathcal{C}({B},\hskip 1.5pt{C})\to\mathcal{U}

pointed at
\mathfrak{S}\mathrel{:}\mathrm{CommSq}_{(f,g)}(X)
.
We define (in curried form)

R\mathrel{:}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Pi$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$({f}^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{C}))\,({g}^{\prime}\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({B},\hskip 1.5pt{C}))$}}\;{(f={f}^{\prime})\to(g={g}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{CommSq}_{({f}^{\prime},{g}^{\prime})}(X)\to\mathcal{U}}

such that
R({f}^{\prime},{g}^{\prime},e_{f},e_{g})(k_{A},k_{B},\delta)
is the type

\displaystyle\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(e_{A}\mathrel{:}m_{A}=k_{A})\,(e_{B}\mathrel{:}m_{B}=k_{B})$}}\hskip 1.0pt,

\displaystyle\gamma=(e_{f}\mathbin{\ast}m_{A})\mathbin{\cdot}({f}^{\prime}\mathbin{\ast}e_{A})\mathbin{\cdot}\delta\mathbin{\cdot}{({g}^{\prime}\mathbin{\ast}e_{B})}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}{(e_{g}\mathbin{\ast}m_{B})}^{-1},

pointed at
(\mathrm{refl}_{m_{A}},\mathrm{refl}_{m_{B}},\mathrm{refl}_{\gamma})\mathrel{:}R(f,g,\mathrm{refl}_{f},\mathrm{refl}_{g},\mathfrak{S})
.
Then it’s enough to show that
\Sigma\,{(\mathrm{CommSq}_{(f,g)}(X))}{\,{R(f,g,\mathrm{refl}_{f},\mathrm{refl}_{g})}}
is contractible, which we have already done in the proof of
Proposition

3.1.4
.
∎

The following somewhat ad hoc-looking technical result is used repeatedly later.

Proposition 3.1.17.

For any commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

in a 2-coherent wild category, the following equalities in
\mathrm{CommSq}_{(\mathrm{id},g)}(X)
hold:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,

where
e\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}{\alpha}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}(\lambda\mathbin{\cdot}{\rho}^{-1})\mathbin{\ast}m_{B}\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha
.

Proof.

Using
Proposition

3.1.4
, to show that
(m_{A},m_{B},\gamma)=(g\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B},m_{B},\lambda)
observe that

{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}\gamma\mathrel{:}m_{A}=g\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B}

and that

\gamma=(\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\ast}({\lambda}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}\gamma))\mathbin{\cdot}\lambda

by properties of whiskering and the coherence in
Proposition

2.2.8
.
To show that
(g\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B},m_{B},\lambda)=(g\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B},\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B},e)
, take
{\lambda}^{-1}\mathrel{:}m_{B}=\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}m_{B}
and observe that

\lambda=e\mathbin{\cdot}(g\mathbin{\ast}\lambda)

by properties of whiskering and the triangle coherators.
∎

3.2.  Pullbacks

Definition 3.2.1.

Pullbacks
.

Let

\mathfrak{P}\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hskip 4.30554pt\hbox to78.77pt{\vbox to51.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.2114pt\lower-27.7208pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

with source
P
, in a wild category
\mathcal{C}
.
By specializing the precomposition map (
Definition

3.1.9
) at
\mathfrak{P}
, we obtain the family of maps

\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{\_}\_\mathrel{:}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Pi$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0})$}}\;{\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{P})\to\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)}.

We say that
\mathfrak{P}
is a
pullback of
\mathfrak{c}
if
(\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{\_}\_)
is a family of equivalences, and a
weak pullback of
\mathfrak{c}
if
(\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{\_}\_)
is a family of retractions, or split surjections.

Corollary 3.2.2.

Universal property of (weak) pullbacks
.

By the characterization of equality of
\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(X)
(
Proposition

3.1.4
), for each
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
and commuting square
\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}(m_{A},m_{B},\gamma)
on
\mathfrak{c}
with source
X
, the fiber of
(\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}\_)
at
\mathfrak{S}
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Sigma$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(m\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{P}))\,(e_{A}\mathrel{:}\pi_{A}\mathbin{\diamond}m=m_{A})\,(e_{B}\mathrel{:}\pi_{B}\mathbin{\diamond}m=m_{B})$}}\hskip 1.0pt,

\displaystyle{\alpha}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}(\mathfrak{p}\mathbin{\ast}m)\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha=(f\mathbin{\ast}e_{A})\mathbin{\cdot}\gamma\mathbin{\cdot}{(g\mathbin{\ast}e_{B})}^{-1}.

Thus
\mathfrak{P}
is a pullback (respectively, a weak pullback) when this type is contractible (respectively, pointed) for every
X
and
\mathfrak{S}
.

Being a pullback is evidently a property: for any cospan
\mathfrak{c}
and
P\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, the predicate

\mathrm{is\text{-}pullback}_{\mathfrak{c},\,P}(\mathfrak{P})\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\raisebox{-1.43994pt}{$\Pi$}\,{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$(X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0})$}}\;{\mathrm{is\text{-}equiv}\,(\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}\_)}

on
\mathrm{CommSq}_{\mathfrak{c}}(P)
is propositional.

Proposition 3.2.3.

Pullbacks are closed under transpose
.

{\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathrm{T}}
is a (weak) pullback if
\mathfrak{P}
is.

Proof.

By a straightforward calculation,

({\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}\_)=({\_}^{\mathrm{T}})\mathbin{\circ}(\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}\_)

for all
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
Since
{\_}^{\mathrm{T}}
is an equivalence (
Proposition

3.1.6
),
({\mathfrak{P}}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}\_)
is an equivalence (respectively, a retraction) when
(\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}\_)
is.
∎

Proposition 3.2.4.

Identity pullbacks
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a 2-coherent wild category, then for all
A,B\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
and
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{B})
the commuting square

\mathfrak{I}_{f}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hbox to77.4pt{\vbox to50.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.69786pt\lower-26.35968pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-38.69786pt}{-19.49306pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -53.55 -26.97)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}{}}{}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

is a pullback.

Proof.

For any
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, a straightforward calculation shows that the map

\mathfrak{I}_{f}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}\_\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({X},\hskip 1.5pt{A})\to\mathrm{CommSq}_{(\mathrm{id},f)}(X)

has retraction
\mathrm{snd}
.
By
Proposition

3.1.17
, this retraction is also a section, i.e.

\mathfrak{I}_{f}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}_{X}k\equiv(f\mathbin{\diamond}k,\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\diamond}k,{\alpha}^{-1}\mathbin{\cdot}((\lambda\mathbin{\cdot}{\rho}^{-1})\mathbin{\ast}k)\mathbin{\cdot}\alpha)=(h,k,\gamma)

for any
(h,k,\gamma)\mathrel{:}\mathrm{CommSq}_{(\mathrm{id},f)}(X)
.
∎

Remark 3.2.5.

In the notation of
Proposition

3.2.4
,
Proposition

3.1.17
says that any commuting square
(m_{A},m_{B},\gamma)
on a cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\hskip 2.2604pt}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 3.01389ptg}

is equal to
\mathfrak{I}_{g}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.96002pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\boxempty$}}m_{B}
.

The next lemma is inspired by the proof of
[
AKL15
, Proposition 4.1.11]
and used in the proof of the pullback pasting lemma (
Lemma

3.2.7
).

Lemma 3.2.6.

Pasting maps of (weak) pullbacks
.

A commuting square
\mathfrak{P}
in a 2-coherent wild category
\mathcal{C}
is a pullback (respectively, a weak pullback) if and only if the vertical pasting map
\frac{\textnormal{--}}{\mathfrak{P}}
(
Definition

3.1.8
) is a family of equivalences (respectively, retractions).

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{P}\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}({g}^{\prime},j,\mathfrak{p})
be a commuting square on
(k,g)
as in
Definition

3.1.8
.
For any
A\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
,
f\mathrel{:}\mathcal{C}({A},\hskip 1.5pt{B})
and
X\mathrel{:}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
, the fiber of
\frac{\textnormal{--}}{\mathfrak{P}}_{A,f,X}
at

\mathfrak{X}\hskip 4.30554pt\mathbin{\vcentcolon\equiv}\hskip 4.30554pt\hbox to89.44pt{\vbox to50.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.36348pt\lower-26.70193pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
